DRAFT · Draft for review. Not for implementation.
CDC · AIRA CDC HL7 v2 Guide (Release 2)
Contents Glossary Quality review

Table of contents

Quality review

These findings come from automated checks run on every build, and from editorial review. They point to places where the draft may be unclear, inconsistent, or hard to use, for the authors to consider. Each finding has a permanent ID, and it also appears on the section it concerns. The example messages are checked against the IGAMT profiles too. A check for duplication across sections will be added in a later build.

Changes from Release 1.5: what changed from the 2018 guide, element by element in the conformance profiles and item by item in the narrative and field notes, with what was not carried forward.

Narrative and IGAMT structure comparison: does the text match the conformance profiles? Answers to the Word review comments, conflicts, change requests for IGAMT, and decisions for CDC. Download all findings (CSV).

TAB Comparison: does the guide fold in each published Technical Advisory Bulletin, without missing or contradicting anything? Requirement by requirement, one page per TAB.

SISC Guidance: the same check for the SISC guidance documents published before the TABs.

MIROW Documents: where the MIROW best practice guides connect with the guide, principle by principle: does the guide carry what each practice needs, and say the same thing?

Proposed Analyses: further comparisons and checks proposed for this draft, such as alignment with the MIROW best practice guides and a trace from Release 1.5, for CDC to consider.

945 findings: 16 issues, 240 warnings, 689 notes.

    qf-no-actor-114ebaca
  • Note 5.9.2 Contradiction vs. Constraint: Its subject is "Or invert National IG behavior", not a system or party, so it is unclear who must comply. Naming the responsible party (for example, "Submitting systems SHALL …" or "IIS SHALL …") makes the requirement testable.
    Requirements that prohibit, redefine, or invert National IG behavior SHALL be treated as contradictions, not extensions.
    qf-no-actor-3069a326
  • Note 5.9.2 Contradiction vs. Constraint: Its subject is "Local constraints", not a system or party, so it is unclear who must comply. Naming the responsible party (for example, "Submitting systems SHALL …" or "IIS SHALL …") makes the requirement testable.
    Local constraints SHOULD be designed to remain compliant with National IG behavior whenever possible.
    qf-no-actor-fc83e5fe

tab-review 87

Reviewed comparison of a Technical Advisory Bulletin (TAB) with the guide.

Show 87 findings
  • Issue 3.2.3 Reporting Immunizations: Single vs. Multiple Administered Vaccinations per VXU: TAB Scenarios for Updates and Queries (Executive Summary; Table 1: Vaccination administered during an encounter): Conflict. The guide says a submitting system SHALL support at least one approach and "MAY support both approaches if operationally required". The TAB says SHALL NOT support both.
    ConflictAction: CDC decision
    TAB A submitting system SHALL support one but SHALL NOT support both: a VXU with one vaccination, if reporting each vaccination individually, or a VXU with all vaccines given during the encounter, if reporting the whole encounter.
    Recommendation CDC and SISC decide. If the guide's MAY stands, it changes a published TAB and should say so; otherwise change s2 to "A submitting system SHALL NOT support both approaches."

    See the TAB comparison

    qf-tab-sc-01
  • Issue 3.3.3.4 ERR-5: Application Error Code: TAB Acknowledgment (ACK) Messaging: Updated Specifications and Guidance (ERR-5 Values): Missing. The narrative refers to "a national code set" but never names it, links to it, or includes it. The guide's own SHALL, SHOULD, and MAY (implement High, Medium, and Low priority codes) cannot be followed without it, because the narrative does not say which codes have which priority. The value set bound to ERR-5 in chapter 7 (HL70533_IZ01) is the 2017 code list plus 5044 and has no priorities. This site now shows the set (version 2026-07-06, pending publication by AIRA) as Appendix D, linked from 3.3.3.4; the narrative still needs to name and cite it.
    MissingAction: Narrative and IGAMT
    TAB ERR-5 carries a code from the national code set "as provided in the National Set of ERR-5 Application Error Codes for Immunization ACK Messaging".
    Recommendation Name and cite the national set in 3.3.3.4 and Resources (once AIRA posts it), and carry Appendix D, the codes with their priorities, into the guide.

    See the TAB comparison

    qf-tab-ack-23
  • Issue 3.3.3.4 ERR-5: Application Error Code: TAB Acknowledgment (ACK) Messaging: Updated Specifications and Guidance (ERR-5 Specifications): Conflict. The narrative matches the TAB, but the profiles do not allow it. HL70533_IZ01 is marked closed and has 67 codes (1-7, 2000-2701, and 5044). The national set (Appendix D) has 280: 213 of them, including 16 of the 20 High priority codes (such as 2018, vaccination date before birth, and 3019, date of birth missing), are not in the value set, so an IIS that meets the SHALL sends ERR-5 values the profile does not allow. The value set also still lists 2202, 2203, and 2502, which the national set has deprecated.
    ConflictAction: IGAMT
    TAB IIS SHALL implement High priority codes, SHOULD implement Medium priority codes, and MAY implement Low priority codes.
    Recommendation Update HL70533_IZ01 in IGAMT to the national set (or make it open and point to the national set), and record the version of the set that the guide adopts.

    See the TAB comparison

  • Warning 3.3.1 IIS Core Intent and Message Disposition: TAB Acknowledgment (ACK) Messaging: Updated Specifications and Guidance (Summary: What Can Be Inferred from the ACK? (Key Takeaways)): Partly covered. The guide says rejection does not imply that nothing was stored (s4), and that resubmission means a corrected message. It never says the whole message is resent, or that submitters should not try to work out what was stored, which is the practical consequence the TAB draws from s4.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    TAB For a rejected (unsuccessful) VXU, the submitter corrects the issue(s) and resubmits the full message. Submitters shall not attempt to ascertain whether certain elements of the patient's IIS record were updated; there is no standardized way for an IIS to say which elements were stored.
    Recommendation Add to 3.3.1: "After a rejection, the submitting system SHALL correct and resubmit the complete message and SHALL NOT attempt to determine which parts of it were stored."

  • Note 3.3.1 IIS Core Intent and Message Disposition: TAB Acknowledgment (ACK) Messaging: Updated Specifications and Guidance (Acceptance and Processing Terms: IIS Core Intent): Reworded. The guide's narrative repeats the TAB's definition, but its statements define acceptance and rejection through "Critical Data", a term the TAB does not use: accepted when the patient is associated "and all submitted Critical Data is processed and stored". The two agree if Critical Data means the patient's identity plus each submitted vaccination; the TAB's examples (one of three immunizations stored: reject; TB result not stored: accept) fit that reading. Because Critical Data is otherwise left to local policy (see tab-ack-g03), the statements can be read more broadly than the TAB.
    RewordedAction: CDC decision
    TAB Accept: the IIS received the message; the patient was associated or created and their shot(s), if submitted, were recorded. Reject: the patient was not associated or created and/or one or more submitted shots were not recorded; a corrected message must be resubmitted.
    Recommendation Confirm that Critical Data is meant to equal the TAB's core intent, and say so in the Critical Data key term, or phrase s1 and s2 in the TAB's terms (patient associated or created, each submitted vaccination recorded).

mirow-review 65

Reviewed comparison of a MIROW best practice guide with the guide: where they connect, and whether they agree.

Show 65 findings
  • Warning 1.4 Key Terms: MIROW guide MIROW Common Vocabulary (Administered/Historical Indicator): Partly covered. The guide has two definitions that don't match. Key Terms defines an administered vaccination as "a dose reported by the system that directly recorded the vaccination event and knows its details". That is about who recorded it. Administered Vaccinations says administered vaccinations "SHALL represent doses given directly by the reporting organization", which is about who gave it. MIROW and RXA-9 code 00 (new immunization record) go with the second. Under the Key Terms wording, a clinic that types another clinic's dose into its own EHR "directly recorded" it.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice The type of a submission, based on whether it is submitted by the vaccinating organization ("administered") or by a third party ("historical"). Administered means the provider organization recorded or submitted its own vaccination event, attesting that it conducted it. Historical means it submitted a vaccination event conducted by a different provider organization.
    Recommendation Change the Key Terms definition of Administered Vaccination to match Administered Vaccinations and MIROW: a dose reported by, or on behalf of, the organization that gave it. Define Historical Vaccination as any dose reported by another party.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cv-02
  • Warning 2.7.4 Entering (Recording) Organization (ORC-17): MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR001 - Minimum/mandatory data elements (BR001 table)): Partly covered. Most of the list is required in the guide (MSH-4, PID-5, PID-7, RXA-3, RXA-5, RXA-9, and RXA-11 for administered doses). Two elements differ. The recording organization, mandatory for a historical submission in MIROW, is ORC-17 in the guide, which is RE and which the guide says IIS "generally do not treat ... as a condition for message acceptance", adding that its usage may be reconsidered if adoption stays limited. The Vital Records elements (birth certificate number, birth facility) are not discussed; Vital Records feeds are outside the guide's main use cases.
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice Every submission names the submitting organization, the patient's first and last names, and date of birth. An administered vaccination also names the vaccinating organization, and a historical one the recording organization; both have the vaccination event date, the vaccine type, and the administered/historical indicator. A demographic record from Vital Records also has the birth certificate number, birth facility, and gender.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether the guide should expect ORC-17 for historical vaccinations, as MIROW does, or say why not. Either way, the ORC-17 section should note that MIROW treats the recording organization as mandatory for historical submissions.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-dqa-04
  • Warning 2.8.2 Date of Birth (PID-7): MIROW guide Consolidating Demographic Records and Vaccination Event Records (BR701, BR5301 - Elements with a single value): Partly covered. The profiles allow one value for each of these except the lot number: RXA-15 is required and repeats (1..*) in the administered RXA, and the guide does not say what a second lot number means. The SISC comparison found the same gap (sisc-lot-01).
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative and IGAMT
    Best practice Some elements must have a single value: for the patient, date of birth, multiple birth indicator, birth order, birthing facility, and birth state; for a vaccination event, the administration date, product, manufacturer, lot number, expiration date, dose volume and unit, site, route, and ordering and administering providers.
    Recommendation State that RXA-15 carries the lot number of the unit administered, and either limit it to one repetition or define what further repetitions carry (for example, a diluent or a separately supplied adjuvant).

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cr-08
  • Warning 2.8.3 Date of Vaccination (RXA-3): MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR110 - Valid calendar dates in a submission): Missing. The guide requires RXA-3 to be the calendar date of administration but does not say what to send when only the month and year of a historical dose are known, which is common with vaccine cards and patient recall.
    MissingAction: CDC decision
    Best practice A date in a submission should be a valid calendar date. Complete dates are best, but if the day of a date is not available, the 15th of the month can be submitted.
    Recommendation CDC to decide how a historical vaccination with an unknown day is reported (the 15th of the month, following MIROW BR110, or a month-precision date), and say it in Historical Vaccinations or Date of Vaccination (RXA-3).

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-dqa-10
  • Warning 3.2.4.2 Non-Identifying Vaccination Fields: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR127 - Vaccination event dosage): Partly covered. RXA-6 and RXA-7 are required in the administered RXA flavor, but the narrative says nothing about either beyond listing dose volume as a non-identifying field, and does not say how to send an unknown amount. MIROW's remark relies on the Release 1.5 convention of 999.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice An administered vaccination should have a dosage that is a positive number with a unit of volume (for example, mL). A value of 999 in HL7 means the amount was not filled in.
    Recommendation State in the guide that RXA-6 carries the dose volume with its unit in RXA-7, and that 999 is sent when the amount is unknown (or decide otherwise), consistent with MIROW BR127.

  • Warning 3.2.4.2 Non-Identifying Vaccination Fields: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR129, BR130, BR132 - Lot number content (Appendix K)): Missing. The guide names lot number quality as a problem conformance cannot solve but says nothing about what RXA-15 should contain. RXA-15 may repeat in the administered flavor (for a unit-of-sale and a unit-of-use lot), which fits BR130 only if each repetition holds one lot number. ERR-5 code 3554 (lot number invalid) exists to report violations.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice A lot number should contain only letters, digits, and dashes (no spaces around a dash); hold a single lot number and nothing else; and not be prefixed, appended, or embedded with extraneous strings.
    Recommendation Add to Non-Identifying Vaccination Fields (or the RXA-15 field notes) that each repetition of RXA-15 carries exactly one lot number as printed on the product, with no other text, and point to MIROW BR129-BR132 and Appendix K.

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Warning 3.3.3.4 ERR-5: Application Error Code: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (Comparison of past MIROW data quality best practices (published with the guide)): Conflict. The national ERR-5 code set cites MIROW rules by their old numbers. Seven codes cite numbers that now mean something else: 2001 (BR 118, now BR115), 2010 (BR 116, now BR126), 2016 and 2017 (BR 119, now BR128), 2018 (BR 101, now BR111), 2019 (BR 103, now BR113), and 2022 (BR102, now BR114). Three more cite numbers that do not match the code's content: 2214 cites BR 114 for the provider-type check (old BR113, now BR168), 2215 cites BR 130 for the license date range (old BR120, now BR133), and 2217 cites BR 113 for the patient name type. Code 2023 keeps the retired 14-day submission rule, which MIROW and this guide now put at 24 hours. Code 2024 cites a rule (medical contraindication) that MIROW retired.
    ConflictAction: CDC decision
    Best practice The 2022 guide renumbered the business rules of the retired 2008 and 2013 data quality guides; its companion comparison maps each old rule to its replacement or marks it retired. For example, old BR118 (date after lot expiration) is now BR115, BR119 (route and site) is BR128, and BR115 (submission within 14 days) became BR106 (within 24 hours).
    Recommendation Ask AIRA, which maintains the code set, to cite the 2022 rule numbers (written "DQA2022 BRnnn"), to align code 2023 with the 24-hour expectation, and to decide whether code 2024 is still needed.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-dqa-45
  • Warning 4.5 Patient Information: MIROW guide Consolidating Demographic Records and Vaccination Event Records (BR1201, BR6001, P10 - Unmerge, and prevent re-merging (twins)): Missing. Unmerging is IIS processing, but the message is where twins are told apart: the multiple birth indicator (PID-24, RE) and birth order (PID-25, conditional). The narrative never mentions them, so submitters have no reason to send them. MIROW also treats them as values from Vital Records, the definitive source (BR601).
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice An IIS should be able to unmerge a consolidated record and should prevent re-merging records it has unmerged, which happens often with twins.
    Recommendation Add a short section on multiple births to Patient Information: send PID-24 and PID-25 whenever known, because they are what keeps an IIS from merging twins, and query with them when known.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cr-09
  • Warning 4.5 Patient Information: MIROW guide MIROW Principles and Business Rules Reports (RR2009 - BR401 - Who is included in reminder/recall, including opt-out): Missing. PD1-11 (publicity code) carries the patient's reminder/recall preference. It is RE in the VXU, with twelve required national codes for what is allowed: none, reminder or recall only, by any method or no calls, or to the provider. PD1-18 carries its date. The narrative never mentions PD1-11. Submitters can't tell what the codes mean, that the field is separate from PD1-12 (sharing) and from patient status, or that an empty field means no preference was recorded. MIROW keeps opting out of reminder/recall separate from patient status (MPS2019 P313, P314; see mirow-mps-11).
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice Criteria for including people in reminder/recall include age, the provider association (immunization home), patient status at both levels, vaccines and doses due, risk status, address, program, insurance, exemptions and contraindications, language, and opting out of reminder/recall in whole or in part. The CDC-endorsed "reminder/ recall status" (publicity code) records whether a person wishes to be contacted.
    Recommendation Add a short section on PD1-11 to Patient Information. Say that it records the patient's or guardian's explicit reminder/recall preference, with the date in PD1-18. Say that it is sent only when a preference was expressed, that it is independent of PD1-12 and of patient status, and that IIS use it to filter reminder/recall (MIROW RR2009 BR401).

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Warning 4.5.1.5 Identifier Changes, Merges, and Retirement: MIROW guide Consolidating Demographic Records and Vaccination Event Records (BR202, BR203 - Retain all past IIS patient IDs; use the current one): Partly covered. The guide requires the current SR or LR in every RSP (as BR203 asks), but says retired identifiers "may be retained as aliases, but a complete identifier history is not required". Submitters are told they can store the IIS identifier (from code 5044 or an RSP) and use it in later queries; after a merge, the one they hold may be retired. The guide does not say whether a query naming a retired SR or LR finds the merged record.
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice All past IIS patient IDs associated with a consolidated record should be retained. The current IIS patient ID should be used in all IIS-originated communications, and in response to a query the IIS should let the submitter know that the IIS patient ID has changed.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether an IIS SHOULD resolve a retired SR or LR in QPD-3 to the current record (which MIROW BR202 makes possible), and to say so in Identifier Changes, Merges, and Retirement.

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Warning 4.5.7.1 Provider-Reported Status: MIROW guide Management of Patient Status in Immunization Information Systems (Chapter 7, Table 8 - Coding provider-level status in HL7): Missing. The guide's patient status sections describe status only in general terms and never name PD1-16 or PD1-17, or say which code means what. The value set (HL70441) has the codes, with 'A' and 'I' required and 'L', 'M', 'P', and 'U' permitted, but a reader of the narrative cannot tell that 'M' is MIROW's "no longer a patient" or that 'P' signals a death.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice Provider-level status is sent in PD1-16 (immunization registry status), with its date in PD1-17: 'A' active, 'I' inactive (reason unspecified), 'L' inactive (lost to follow-up), 'M' inactive (no longer a patient), and 'P' permanently inactive, which MIROW reads as deceased once death is confirmed.
    Recommendation In Provider-Reported Status, name PD1-16 and PD1-17 and give MIROW's meaning for each HL70441 code (Table 8), including how 'P' relates to the deceased status.

  • Warning 4.5.7.3 Deceased Status: MIROW guide Management of Patient Status in Immunization Information Systems (BR421, chapter 7 - Deceased): Partly covered. The guide makes deceased status take precedence and says it may be reported by providers or external death reporting, but does not name PID-29 and PID-30, or say how a death is reported in a VXU.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice Status becomes inactive (deceased) at both levels only when the death is confirmed. A death sent in PID-29 (date of death) and PID-30 (death indicator) is a confirming message from the provider; a 'P' in PD1-16 without PID-30 'Y' should be investigated, and reminder/recall suppressed meanwhile.
    Recommendation In Deceased Status, state that a provider reports a death in PID-30 ('Y') with the date in PID-29, and point to MIROW BR421 for how an IIS confirms it.

  • Warning 4.6.2.2 CVX - Vaccines Administered Code: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR120 - Combination vaccine reported as single vaccination event): Missing. The guide does not say this for U.S. combination vaccines. The only statement on combinations is the opposite case: a foreign combination product with no CVX code SHOULD be decomposed into its component antigens, one vaccination event each. Without the general rule, a reader could take the foreign-product rule as the norm.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice A combination vaccine dose should be reported as a single vaccination event (MMRV as one dose of MMRV, not a dose of MMR and a dose of varicella).
    Recommendation In the CVX section, state that a combination vaccine is reported as one RXA with the combination's CVX (MIROW BR120), and make clear that decomposing into components applies only to foreign products with no CVX code.

  • Warning 4.6.2.4.3 NDC Requirements for Administered Vaccinations: MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (BR704 (remarks), chapter 7 - Multi-component vaccines): Partly covered. The guide tells submitters to report the NDC of the lyophilized powder for reconstituted vaccines, and any NDC for other multi-component products. It says nothing about which lot number goes in RXA-15. RXA-15 repeats (1..*) in the administered RXA, so a submitter could send every component's lot, the outer package's, or one chosen at random. MIROW's answer is one lot number and one NDC from the outside of the package. The guide's NDC rule picks the powder, which serves product identification but not inventory. The same RXA-15 gap was found in the SISC and consolidation comparisons (sisc-lot-01, mirow-cr-08).
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice Each component of a multi-component vaccine (one with a diluent or components that are combined) has its own lot number. There is always one lot number and one NDC, on the outside of the package, that characterize the vaccine as a whole, and those are used for inventory. For a product such as Pentacel, with separate NDCs for powder and liquid, the only NDC that can be used for ordering, inventory, and returns is the one on the CDC contract.
    Recommendation CDC to decide which lot number a submitter sends in RXA-15 for a vaccine with several components: the one on the outer package that the IIS inventory knows (MIROW BR704), or the active component's, to match the NDC rule. Say so with the RXA-15 cardinality fix.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-im-03
  • Warning 4.6.4.1 Identifying Field Changes: MIROW guide Vaccination Level Deduplication in Immunization Information Systems (BR10 - Different lot numbers): Partly covered. The guide classifies lot number as non-identifying: a corrected lot number is sent as an update and SHOULD NOT by itself make the IIS treat the vaccination as new. That works when the IIS finds the earlier report through the submitting source and ORC-3. The guide says ORC-3 matching is not yet consistent, though, and an IIS that falls back to MIROW evaluation will see two records with different lot numbers and, under BR10, keep both. The two documents are not in conflict, but the guide does not say how they fit together.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice If two records have different lot numbers, they are most likely different vaccination events (allowing for typos and generic entries such as 9999). A matching lot number is not decisive.
    Recommendation In IIS Matching and Correction Processing, say that an update is applied to the source record it identifies (by source and ORC-3, or the identifying fields) before deduplication is considered, so that MIROW's lot number rule does not turn a lot number correction into a second event.

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Note 2.3.3 Record Consolidation and Deduplication: MIROW guide Immunization Information Systems Patient-Level De-duplication Best Practices (Table 1.1, 5.2.1 (6) - Err on the side of preventing false merges): Reworded. The guide says high-confidence matches may be merged automatically and ambiguous cases reviewed, but not that a duplicate is the preferred error. The same point was made for vaccination events (see mirow-vd-04, whose recommendation should apply to patients as well).
    Reworded
    Best practice When in doubt, practitioners should prevent false merges. Wrongly merging two patients' records has more severe consequences than leaving one patient duplicated.

  • Note 2.3.3 Record Consolidation and Deduplication: MIROW guide Vaccination Level Deduplication in Immunization Information Systems (BR09, P04 - Different until proven duplicate): Reworded. The guide says high-confidence matches may be merged automatically and ambiguous ones queued for manual review, but not that, when in doubt, keeping both records is the safer error. That bias matters to submitters, who otherwise may expect every resend to collapse into one record.
    RewordedAction: Narrative
    Best practice Records are assumed to be different until proven to be duplicates, because a false match loses immunization data while a false non-match only leaves a duplicate. When the evaluation cannot decide, the records go to manual review.
    Recommendation Add to Record Consolidation and Deduplication that IIS deliberately prefer to keep two records rather than wrongly merge them (MIROW BR09), so occasional duplicates are expected.

    See the MIROW comparison

    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice All principles and business rules from the ten MIROW guides, with their current wording, numbered by guide and year, with examples and remarks, and with terms from the MIROW Common Vocabulary marked.

  • Note 2.6.2 Shared Responsibility for Data Quality: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (Introduction; Chapter 2 (data quality throughout the IIS lifecycle)): Reworded. The guide's data quality sections say the same thing in their own words and cite this MIROW guide as reference [5], but under the title "AIRA's IIS Data Quality Best Practices", which is not its title, and without saying that it is a MIROW best practice guide.
    RewordedAction: Narrative
    Best practice Data quality in an IIS is achieved by several approaches together (programmatic and technical), across onboarding, incoming data submissions, and data at rest. This guide is AIRA's best practice reference for it, and replaces the 2008 and 2013 MIROW data quality guides and the 2014 lot number micro-guide.
    Recommendation Change reference [5] to the guide's title, "Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems" (MIROW, 2022), and name it in Data Quality Processes as the community's best practices for onboarding, incoming data, and data at rest.

  • Note 2.7 Identifying Organizations: MIROW guide MIROW Common Vocabulary (Submitter, Recorder, Vaccinator, IIS-Authorized Organization; notes on provider organization and IIS-AO): Reworded. The guide's roles line up with MIROW's: submitter is MSH-4, vaccinator is RXA-11.4, and recorder is ORC-17 (which the guide names "Entering (Recording) Organization"). The IIS assigns every organization identifier. The guide adds the responsible organization (MSH-22) and keeps the "accountable" wording MIROW questioned. When a message passes through an intermediary (an HIE, for example), MSH-4 names the organization on the connection. The message has no place for the other organizations in MIROW's chain of submission, so the IIS has to know them from onboarding.
    Reworded
    Best practice An IIS-authorized organization (IIS-AO) has an agreement with an IIS to submit or retrieve information, and always has an IIS-AO ID assigned by the IIS. In a submission an IIS-AO plays one or more roles: recorder (records the information), vaccinator (vaccinates the patient), or submitter (submits to the IIS, or to an intermediary with the IIS as destination). One IIS-AO may submit for another. The IIS needs to know every IIS-AO in the chain of submission. MIROW also notes that the CDC-endorsed definition of responsible organization ("originated and is accountable for the content") should change, because not every IIS-AO is accountable for the content.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cv-03
  • Note 2.7.5 Administering Organization (RXA-11): MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (BR706 - Organization responsible for the inventory): Partly covered. The guide defines the administering organization in RXA-11 as "the entity that physically administered the vaccine and managed its inventory", which assumes the two are the same. MSH-22 identifies the organization responsible for the data, not the inventory. Where they differ, nothing in the message says whose inventory to decrement. MIROW accepts that the IIS works this out from what it knows of the organizations.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice The provider organization responsible for the inventory must be associated with every dose administered. It may differ from the organization that gave the vaccine, for example when a central depot redistributes vaccine. A report without it can still be used for inventory if the IIS can determine the responsible organization from what was submitted (that is, not an umbrella organization).
    Recommendation In Administering Organization (RXA-11), drop "and managed its inventory" or qualify it. Note that the inventory may belong to another organization (MIROW BR706), which the IIS determines from its records of the submitter's organizations rather than from the message.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-im-07
  • Note 2.7.6 Parent and Child Organizations: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR173 to BR175 - IIS-AO IDs; P21 - Complete chain of submitting organizations): Reworded. The guide makes the IIS the authority for unique organization identifiers and gives each role its own field (MSH-4 submitting, MSH-22 responsible, ORC-17 recording, RXA-11 administering). Its model of the "chain" is narrower than P21: one submitting organization per authenticated connection, with intermediaries such as HIEs transparent. That is a reasonable message-level reading of P21, but the guide does not say so, and it does not carry BR174's advice against embedding changeable information in identifiers.
    Reworded
    Best practice Each IIS-AO has a unique ID that is never reused and does not embed information that can change (relationships, location); submitting organizations should include valid IIS-AO IDs for all IIS-AOs involved; and a submission should identify the complete chain of submitting organizations.
    Recommendation In Implementation and Maintenance Considerations, add that identifiers should not embed information that can change, such as location or parent organization (MIROW BR174).

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Note 2.8.2 Date of Birth (PID-7): MIROW guide Consolidating Demographic Records and Vaccination Event Records (BR601 - Supremacy of vital statistics): Reworded. Consistent with the guide's use of official documentation for the date of birth; the guide does not single out Vital Records or cover the other birth elements (see mirow-cr-09).
    Reworded
    Best practice Vital statistics is the definitive source for date of birth, gender, multiple birth indicator, birth order, birthing facility, birth state, mother's maiden name, and birth certificate number.

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Note 2.8.2 Date of Birth (PID-7): MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR151 - Minimum date of birth; BR152 - Date of birth default): Missing. Note that MIROW's default for an unknown day is the 1st for a date of birth (BR152) but the 15th for other dates (BR110).

    Best practice A date of birth should be after January 1, 1900. When a patient does not know their own date of birth, the month defaults to January and the day to 1; this is not a substitute for collecting the date of birth.
    Recommendation In Date of Birth (PID-7), point to MIROW BR152 for patients who do not know their date of birth.

  • Note 3.2.5.3 Modifying Non-Identifying Vaccination Fields: MIROW guide Consolidating Demographic Records and Vaccination Event Records (BR1002, BR5802, P12 - Do not overwrite validated data): Partly covered. The guide says IIS "are expected to apply these updates to the existing vaccination record", without the MIROW exception, although it also says returned values may differ from those submitted and that IIS must not require submitters to "correct" names. A submitter whose update is not applied has no way to know.
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice Consolidation should not overwrite data the IIS has validated. If an IIS corrected a value and the same source submits the same wrong value again, the validated value is kept; the general rule of using the most recent value gives way.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether the guide acknowledges that an IIS may keep a validated value instead of applying a resubmitted one (MIROW BR1002 and BR5802), and if so whether the ACK should report it as a warning.

  • Note 3.2.5.6 Patient Demographic Records and VXUs Without Immunizations: MIROW guide Patient Status in Immunization Information Systems: Recommendations and Implementation Strategies (Section 2 (EHR survey), Appendix D - Status changes are sent only with a vaccination): Partly covered. The guide requires IIS to accept a VXU with no vaccinations, but describes it for "correcting a patient's name, address, or date of birth on an existing immunization record, or updating demographic information in advance of an anticipated vaccination event". It also asks submitters to limit such messages and to use ADT where available. A provider reporting a death, or that a patient has moved or is no longer a patient, is not named as a reason. An EHR reading the guide could conclude it should not send one.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice EHRs send patient status only when an administered immunization is also included. Most trigger a VXU only when a vaccination is given, none send ADT, and if a provider learns a patient has moved or died, that information may never reach the IIS.
    Recommendation In Patient Demographic Records and VXUs Without Immunizations, name reporting a death (PID-29, PID-30) and a change in the provider's patient status (PD1-16, PD1-17) as appropriate uses of a VXU without vaccinations.

The guide supplies the national convention. A placeholder name is marked with name type 'NB' and never sent as the legal name. When the real name is known, the submitter sends both names together, along with the child's complete history, so the IIS can link the records.

    Best practice Nearly all interface data needs pre-screening. IIS may decide not to use records with names like Baby Boy, Baby Girl, BB, or BG, and should remove placeholder words before matching. In the longer term, "placeholder information could be standardized by convention on a national scale".

    Best practice PD1-16 and PD1-17 directly identify the patient status and its date, and PID-30 and PID-29 report a death with its date. The RXA, PD1-11, and PD1-12 can be used to infer status.   • Note 4.6.1.1 Administered Vaccinations: MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (P702, BR704 - A lot number for every administered dose): Reworded. RXA-15 is required in the administered RXA and optional in the historical RXA, which matches MIROW. But the guide does not let an IIS reject a vaccination only because the lot number is missing, unless the IIS has declared it Critical Data. The later MIROW guides take the same position: the 2016 decrementing guide records the vaccination and skips decrementing (S704), and the 2022 data quality guide makes the lot number recommended (BR131). So MIROW's 2012 rejection rule has been superseded.
    Reworded
    Best practice Every administered dose must be documented and reported with its lot number, and a report without one is rejected. Historical doses do not need one. Providers should adopt barcoding and drop-down lists so the lot number is recorded correctly.

  • Note 4.6.1.2 Historical Vaccinations: MIROW guide Vaccination Level Deduplication in Immunization Information Systems (P10, Table 9 - Confidence in the data): Partly covered. The message carries this information: RXA-9 distinguishes a new administration (00) from historical records by source (from another provider, another registry, a written record, a school record, a birth certificate, a public agency, or the patient's recall, which the value set marks as low reliability). The narrative never mentions these codes or asks submitters to send the most specific one rather than 01 (source unspecified).
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice The confidence placed in a record depends on who submitted it and how: a primary submitter reporting administered doses from a clinical system ranks highest, claims data lower, and secondary submitters (historical records) from medium to low, in an order each IIS sets (for example, schools with nurses rank higher).
    Recommendation In Historical Vaccinations, list the RXA-9 historical source codes, ask submitters to send the most specific source they know, and note that IIS use it to weigh records when deduplicating (MIROW P10).

    See the MIROW comparison

    Guide adds
    Best practice (Not addressed in the 2006 guide, which predates bidirectional query.)

  • Note 4.6.2.3 NDC - National Drug Code: MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (P701, BR701, BR702, chapter 7 - NDC supremacy, and finding the NDC from the lot number): Reworded. The guide keeps CVX as the canonical code and treats NDC as a SHOULD for administered doses, valuable for traceability and reconciliation. That fits MIROW's view that the IIS can derive the NDC behind the scenes. The guide accepts unit-of-use as well as unit-of-sale NDCs. An IIS that receives a unit-of-use NDC (the vial or syringe) still has to reach the unit-of-sale NDC for inventory, which MIROW does through the lot number. The guide doesn't say this, but it is IIS processing.
    Reworded
    Best practice Inventory is managed by NDC, because VFC and VTrckS require it. The NDC used is the one received in the shipment file, from the outside of the package (unit of sale). Providers rarely record the NDC when they give a dose. The recommended way for the IIS to find the NDC of an administered dose is from its lot number, which was linked to an NDC when the shipment arrived. Mapping from CVX and MVX without the lot number is not a best practice, and asking providers to record the NDC by hand is not suggested.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-im-02
  • Note 4.6.2.4.1 Accepted Code Systems for Administered Vaccinations: MIROW guide Data Quality Assurance in Immunization Information Systems (BR117 - Vaccine type CVX): Reworded.     Reworded
    Best practice IIS programs should teach submitters that CVX is the preferred way to report the vaccine type, rather than CPT.

    See the MIROW comparison

    Best practice (In 2016 MIROW found that best practice for action codes was not documented, that many EHRs send only "A", and that it was not known whether ORC-3 was populated reliably. It recommended examining both "in more depth in the future to develop a standard of practice".)

  • Note 4.6.4.5 Inventory and Accountability Considerations: MIROW guide Decrementing Inventory via Electronic Data Exchange (BR204, S501 to S503, S1001 - Lot problems): Partly covered. The ACK rules give the right answer: the vaccination was recorded, so the VXU is accepted, and an issue that needs correction is a Warning. ERR-5 has codes for some of these cases: 2001 (expired vaccine administered), 2011 (lot not valid for the vaccine), and the general 2601 (inventory management error). But the guide never says that an inventory problem is reported this way. Submitters cannot tell from the guide whether a dose was decremented. Inventory and Accountability Considerations covers corrections only.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice Only active inventory is decremented. When the lot in a submission is expired, inactive, at zero balance, or not in the provider's inventory at all, the vaccination is still recorded (as non-viable if the lot had expired), the dose is not decremented, and the IIS notifies the provider organization or EHR. P07 lists the ACK as one way to notify, alongside reports and the user interface.
    Recommendation Add to Inventory and Accountability Considerations that when a vaccination is recorded but inventory cannot be decremented (lot not found, inactive, expired, or at zero balance), the IIS SHOULD return a Warning with ERR-5 2601, or a more specific code such as 2001, so the submitter can correct the lot or account for the dose (MIROW BR204, P07).

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-di-12
  • Note 4.6.4.5 Inventory and Accountability Considerations: MIROW guide Decrementing Inventory via Electronic Data Exchange (BR205, S1203, S1204 - Changes after a closed reconciliation): Reworded. The guide allows local policy to delay or review a correction "when inventory or accountability records can no longer be reconciled". The ACK says whether it was applied and whether manual follow-up is needed. An update or delete not applied for policy reasons is reported as Information, not as an Error. One consequence is not spelled out: correcting an identifying field such as RXA-3 means a delete followed by an add, which reverses and repeats the decrement and so can reach into a closed reconciliation period.
    Reworded
    Best practice The IIS should not decrement automatically when the vaccination date falls on or before the end of the most recent closed reconciliation. A delete or a late vaccination for a closed period needs the reconciliation to be reopened by IIS staff, and an issue is logged for manual work.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-di-17
  • Note 4.6.4.5 Inventory and Accountability Considerations: MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (BR718, PM07 - Flag doses that do not match the inventory): Partly covered. The guide's ACK rules support this, but the guide does not say that an unmatched lot is reported. This is the same gap as mirow-di-12.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Best practice When a dose reported by an EHR cannot be matched to a lot in the provider's inventory, the IIS must flag the discrepancy to the provider organization, preferably immediately and in any case before the reconciliation period closes. The submitter of HL7 data should have good visibility of reported vaccinations that did not match known lots, and a simple process to resolve them.
    Recommendation Covered by the recommendation in mirow-di-12, which should cite MIROW 2012 BR718 as well as the 2016 guide.

    See the MIROW comparison

  • Note 4.6.5 Funding Source and Eligibility: MIROW guide MIROW Common Vocabulary (Dose-Level Eligibility, Dose-Level Public/Private Indicator, Lot-Level Public/Private Indicator): Reworded. Covered by the mapping recommended in mirow-cv-01.
    Reworded
    Best practice Dose-level eligibility is a patient's eligibility for a funding program, determined for each administered dose. The dose-level public/private indicator is the source of funding for a dose, assigned by the provider organization (the CDC-endorsed "vaccine funding source"). The lot-level indicator is the IIS's designation of an inventory lot.

  • Note 4.6.5 Funding Source and Eligibility: MIROW guide Decrementing Inventory via Electronic Data Exchange (Introduction; Chapter 9 - HL7 immunization messaging): Missing. The guide says funding information is "primarily used for inventory decrementing and program operations" and that it supports "MIROW-aligned practices", but it cites neither this guide nor the MIROW guide on IIS collaboration with VFC (2011) that it builds on.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Best practice Best practices for decrementing a provider organization's vaccine inventory automatically from the vaccination events it submits electronically, so that inventory tracking and immunization tracking are served by the same submission.
    Recommendation In Funding Source and Eligibility, cite MIROW's Decrementing Inventory via Electronic Data Exchange (2016) as the best practice for using these elements to decrement inventory.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-di-01
  • Note 4.6.5.1 Patient Funding Eligibility (Per Dose): MIROW guide Immunization Information System Inventory Management Operations (BR705 - Patient eligibility for every administered dose): Reworded. Eligibility is reported for each administered dose. A jurisdiction may enforce it for participants in public programs, but the guide prefers accepting the vaccination and following up. The 2016 decrementing guide (S701) and ERR-5 code 2500 take the same approach.
    Reworded
    Best practice Patient eligibility must be documented and reported for every administered dose, and a report without it is rejected. Eligibility is needed to know whether to decrement public or private stock when both hold the same lot. It does not apply to historical doses.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-im-06
  • Note 4.6.5.1 Patient Funding Eligibility (Per Dose): MIROW guide MIROW Principles and Business Rules Reports (DLE2011 - BR614, BR615 - Which eligibility to report when several apply): Conflict. The national value set (V01 to V05 and V22 to V25) has one code per category, and the guide allows at most one value per dose. That supports MIROW's minimum set but not its best practice. The guide does not give the hierarchy for choosing among codes, so a patient who is both on Medicaid and AI/AN may be reported either way.
    ConflictAction: CDC decision
    Best practice The minimum is a single item: Medicaid, AI/AN, uninsured, underinsured (at FQHC, RHC, or deputized providers only), grantee-eligible, or private coverage. When the patient meets more than one VFC criterion, the hierarchy is Medicaid, then AI/AN, then uninsured, then underinsured. The best practice is to report every applicable item in a valid combination (for example, AI/AN and uninsured), with rules for which combinations are valid and how to interpret them.
    Recommendation CDC to confirm that only one eligibility value is sent per dose, which departs from MIROW's best practice (DLE2011 BR615). If so, add to Patient Funding Eligibility (Per Dose) that when more than one applies, the submitter reports the first in MIROW's order: V02 (Medicaid), V04 (AI/AN), V03 (uninsured), V05 (underinsured).

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-pbr-03
  • Note 4.6.5.2 Vaccine Funding Source and Stock Models: MIROW guide Decrementing Inventory via Electronic Data Exchange (Chapter 3 - Vaccine storage models): Partly covered. The guide says U.S. jurisdictions "operate either a two-stock or three-stock inventory model" and that each IIS SHALL select one of the two. The universal model is covered by Universal Purchase and Default Eligibility. The guide does not mention the replacement model, in which the stock used is always private at the time of administration and eligibility alone drives replacement, nor say what funding source such providers send. A multi-stock jurisdiction can use the open value set's local extensions, but the guide does not say so.
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice Awardees use one of four storage models: multi-stock, separated by individual fund type (VFC, 317, CHIP, state, private), which MIROW notes the HL7 funding source value set does not support; three-stock (VFC public, non-VFC public, private); two-stock (public, private); or one-stock. In a one-stock model, the provider either uses private vaccine for everyone and VFC replaces the doses given to eligible children (replacement), or has only public vaccine (universal). In a one-stock model the fund type of a dose is not known when it is given.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether the SHALL in Vaccine Funding Source and Stock Models should allow a one-stock (replacement) model, and if so to say what funding source a provider in that model reports. Also say that fund types beyond the national codes (such as 317, CHIP, or state) are sent as published local extensions.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-di-06
  • Note 4.7.7.4 Dose Validity and Reason for Validity: MIROW guide MIROW Common Vocabulary (Dose Condition): Partly covered. The guide has no way for a submitter to say that a dose it gave was substandard (expired, stored out of range, or given incorrectly). An IIS that learns of it shows it only as an invalid dose in the evaluation of the RSP. MIROW's decrementing guide records such a dose as "administered, non-viable" (S501), and the deduplication guide keeps the repeat dose and flags the invalid one (mirow-vd-15). The usual practice is to report the dose as given and let the IIS or the forecaster judge it, which may be enough.
    Partly coveredAction: CDC decision
    Best practice An indicator that a dose administered to a patient is considered substandard.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether the guide should say how a submitter reports a dose it knows to be substandard (for example, a dose later found to have been stored out of range), or state that this is left to the IIS's evaluation.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cv-12
  • Note 4.8.3 Terminology Clarification: MIROW guide MIROW Common Vocabulary (Immunization Contraindication, Immunization Exemption): Reworded. The guide discourages the unqualified term "contraindication". It treats patient characteristics as conditions and a decision not to give a dose as non-administration. That is a more precise split than MIROW's. A MIROW contraindication is a guide condition, and a MIROW exemption is closest to the guide's refusal.
    Reworded
    Best practice An immunization contraindication is a condition in a patient that greatly increases the chance of a serious adverse reaction and makes a vaccination inadvisable. An immunization exemption is a non-medical reason for excluding a patient from a vaccination.

    See the MIROW comparison

    qf-mirow-cv-06

sisc-review 38

Reviewed comparison of a SISC guidance document (published before the TABs) with the guide.

Show 38 findings
  • Warning 3.2.4.2 Non-Identifying Vaccination Fields: SISC guidance Guidance on Unit of Sale/Unit of Use Lot Numbers (Document Use): Missing. The guide treats the same unit-of-use and unit-of-sale problem for NDCs, and IGAMT makes RXA-15 (lot number) required for administered doses, but the guide never says which lot number RXA-15 carries, or that it may differ from the inventory lot.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance Many systems use the unit-of-sale lot number for ordering and inventory but the unit-of-use lot number at administration. Where the two differ, the lot number recorded for a vaccination is the unit-of-use lot.
    Recommendation State that RXA-15 carries the lot number of the unit administered (the vial or syringe), that for some products this differs from the unit-of-sale lot used for inventory, and link this guidance.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-lot-01
  • Warning 3.3.3.2 ERR-4: Severity Code: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Caveats): Partly covered. The guide defines the ERR-4 severities only in terms of a VXU and its core intent ("results in rejection of the submitted VXU because core intent was not fulfilled"). Nothing says the same severities apply to an RSP, or what "core intent" means for a query.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance The ERR-4 severity definitions in the ACK guidance (Error, Warning, Information) also apply to query responses.
    Recommendation State that the ERR-4 severities apply to query responses as well, and say what an Error means for a query (the query could not be executed and must be corrected and resent).

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-01
  • Warning 3.3.3.4 ERR-5: Application Error Code: SISC guidance Query Responses and Patient Protection (Guidance for incorporation into a future release): Missing. The code now exists in the national set of ERR-5 codes: 2700 "Protected Patient Found" (and 2701 "Potentially Protected Patient Found"), both noted as applying to a single high-threshold match. The guide publishes that code set (Appendix D) but does not mention either code in its consent or Z33 sections.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance In the next release, ERR-5 in the modified Z33 carries a new error code for a protected patient found.
    Recommendation Name ERR-5 codes 2700 and 2701 in the Z33 section, as the way to tell a querying system that a protected patient was found.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-qrpp-05
  • Warning 3.3.4 MSA-1: Derivation of Message-Level Disposition: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (query is successfully processed)): Missing. These rules agree with the guide's MSA-1 rules for VXUs ('AA' with no 'W' or 'E', 'AE' with 'W' and no 'E'), but the guide states them only for a VXU. Nothing in the RSP sections says how to populate MSA-1 in a query response.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance MSA-1 is 'AA' when there are no errors (no ERR segment), and may also be 'AA' with an ERR segment whose ERR-4 is 'I'. MSA-1 is 'AE' when there are errors that do not prevent the search from completing; the ERR segment then has ERR-4 'W', and ERR-4 may not be 'E'.
    Recommendation Say in the RSP sections that MSA-1 and ERR-4 in a query response follow the same rules as in an ACK, or state the RSP rules there.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-08
  • Warning 3.5.2 RSP Response Profiles: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (query is successfully processed); Summary): Missing. The guide never explains QAK-2 or how it relates to MSA-1. The only QAK-2 value it names is 'TM', in the Z31 section. The IGAMT profiles bind QAK-2 to HL70208 (OK, NF, TM, AE, AR) but set no rule linking it to the outcome or to MSA-1.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance QAK-2 reflects the outcome of the search and is independent of MSA-1, which reports the technical processing of the message. In a successful query, QAK-2 may not be 'AR' or 'AE'.
    Recommendation Add a short section, or a table like this guidance's Table 2, on QAK-2: what each value means, which values go with which response profile, and that QAK-2 and MSA-1 are set independently.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-09
  • Warning 3.5.2.1 Z23 - Return an Acknowledgement: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (message is rejected)): Partly covered. The guide's Z23 section describes the same outcome more broadly ("malformed or invalid MSH/QPD segments, missing required message header information, or other unrecoverable errors detected prior to query execution"), and figure 17 shows it as "Structurally valid? No". It does not name the four MSH conditions. The fifth condition, "unrelated to format or content", is one the guide removes as a reason for 'AR' on a VXU (following the ACK TAB), because it describes a transport problem. The guide does not say whether it is also removed for queries.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance The message is rejected when it has an unsupported message type or event code (MSH-9), an unsupported processing ID (MSH-11), or an unsupported version ID (MSH-12), or cannot be processed for reasons unrelated to format or content. The Z23 (ACK) profile is the response.
    Recommendation Name the MSH conditions that lead to a Z23, and say that, as for VXUs, a transport or connectivity problem is never reported with a Z23.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-03
  • Warning 3.5.2.1 Z23 - Return an Acknowledgement: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (message is rejected)): Missing. The Z23 section says the IIS "is unable to execute the query" but not what MSA-1 and ERR-4 contain. The guide's MSA-1 rules ('AR' when there is a severity 'E' error) are written for a VXU ("SHALL be returned to convey VXU rejection"). The IGAMT Z23 profile allows either a Q11 or a V04 trigger, so the same profile serves both, but only the VXU case is described.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance In a Z23 sent in response to a query, MSA-1 must be AR, an ERR segment must be present, and ERR-4 must be E (the sender should correct the issue and resubmit).
    Recommendation In the Z23 section, state that a Z23 sent in response to a QBP has MSA-1 'AR' and one ERR segment with ERR-4 'E'.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-04
  • Warning 3.5.2.2 Z32 - Return Complete Immunization History: SISC guidance Query Responses and Patient Protection (Guidance when implementing Release 1.5): Missing. The guide says a Z32 or Z42 returns "the complete immunization history", but never addresses withholding some doses for privacy, or the clinical risk of a history that looks complete but is not.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance A partial patient history is not recommended. In the absence of widespread support for dose-level protection, all doses should be returned if any doses are returned: a provider who takes a partial history for a full one may make an inappropriate clinical decision, and confidence in the IIS suffers.
    Recommendation In the consent sections or the Z32/Z42 sections, state that an IIS SHOULD NOT return a partial history to protect some doses: it returns either the whole history or a Z33.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-qrpp-02
  • Warning 3.5.2.3 Z31 - Return a List of Candidates: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Summary (Table 2, footnote 1)): Partly covered. The Z31 section says that an IIS with too many candidates "returns a QAK-2 value of TM" rather than an oversized Z31, but does not say which response profile carries it (Z33), and does not allow 'NF' where policy forbids revealing that several patients match.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance More than the maximum number of matches: Z33 with 'TM' (preferred, as it is most specific) or 'NF' (where local law, policy, or regulation prevents indicating that several patients match).
    Recommendation Say that too many matches is reported with a Z33 and QAK-2 'TM', or 'NF' where local law or policy does not allow the IIS to reveal that several patients match.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-12
  • Warning 3.5.2.4 Z33 - Return a Response with No Person Records: SISC guidance Query Responses and Patient Protection (Guidance when implementing Release 1.5): Missing. The guide has a single Z33 for every case and no way to tell a querying system that a protected patient was found. The FAQ records why that matters: EHR systems may need to distinguish a protected patient from no patient (for example, so staff do not create a duplicate record or keep re-querying).
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance Where law and policy do not strictly prohibit indicating that the patient exists, send a modified Z33: MSA-1 'AA'; ERR-3 populated (207, Application Internal Error, suggested); ERR-4 'I' (the querying system is not expected to fix or resend anything); ERR-5 empty; ERR-8 text saying a patient was found but is protected (for example, "A match was found in the IIS but no value is returned since Data Sharing for the record is set to No"); QAK-2 'NF'.
    Recommendation Describe the "protected patient found" Z33 in the Z33 section, updated for this guide: MSA-1 'AA', one ERR segment with ERR-4 'I' and ERR-5 2700 (see sisc-qrpp-05), ERR-8 text for the user, and QAK-2 'NF' (or 'PD'; see sisc-qrpp-06). State that an IIS uses it only where law and policy allow it to reveal that the patient exists.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-qrpp-04
  • Warning 3.5.2.4 Z33 - Return a Response with No Person Records: SISC guidance Query Responses and Patient Protection (Guidance for incorporation into a future release): Missing. Neither the guide nor IGAMT has 'PD': QAK-2 is bound to HL70208_IZ01 with OK, NF, TM, AE, and AR. 'PD' is not an HL7 v2.5.1 code, so adding it would extend an HL7 table. With ERR-5 2700 available, 'PD' may no longer be needed, but the FAQ said it would be added, so its omission should be a decision rather than an oversight.
    MissingAction: CDC decision
    Guidance In the next release, QAK-2 is 'PD' (Protected Data), a new code used when a protected patient is found.
    Recommendation CDC to decide whether to add QAK-2 'PD' or rely on QAK-2 'NF' with ERR-5 2700. Record the decision in the change history.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-qrpp-06
  • Warning 3.5.2.4 Z33 - Return a Response with No Person Records: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (error prevents the query)): Missing. The guide's Z33 section lists only no match, policy limits, and consent restrictions, and says that a Z33 means the IIS "has processed the query". Figure 17 has no path for a query that is accepted but cannot be executed. The combination this guidance requires also conflicts with the guide's new ACK model: for a VXU the guide says MSA-1 'AE' SHALL NOT be returned with a severity 'E' error, and that 'AE' means a qualified acceptance. If that model applies to query responses, a failed query would get MSA-1 'AR'; if it does not, the guide needs to say so.
    MissingAction: CDC decision
    Guidance When the message is accepted but an error prevents the query from being executed, the response is a Z33 with MSA-1 'AE', QAK-2 'AE', and ERR-4 'E'. QAK-2 may not be AR (reserved for the Z23 conditions) or OK, TM, or NF (which report a query that was executed).
    Recommendation CDC to decide how a query that cannot be executed is reported: Z33 with MSA-1 'AE' as in this guidance, or Z33 (or Z23) with MSA-1 'AR' to match the ACK model. Then add the outcome to the Z33 section and to figure 17, with the QAK-2 value ('AE').

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-rsp-06
  • Warning 4.4.2.6 OBX-14 Date/Time of the Observation: SISC guidance Guidance on Populating OBX-14 (Populating OBX-14 (principles)): Reworded. Section 4.4.2.6 says OBX-14 should reflect when the observation "occurred or was recorded", adding "recorded". Section 4.8.6.3 says OBX-14 "represents the date the observation was authored or recorded" and "does not indicate when the underlying condition began", except for ongoing conditions. This guidance's summary is explicit that OBX-14 reflects the time of the observation "and not when the message is created".
    RewordedAction: Narrative
    Guidance OBX-14 should be when the event (observation) occurred. For observations spanning several days (pregnancy, allergies), use the onset. For serology, use the date/time the blood was drawn.
    Recommendation Drop "or recorded" in 4.4.2.6, and align 4.8.6.3 with it: OBX-14 is when the observation occurred (the onset for ongoing conditions). If CDC prefers the recording date for conditions, say so in 4.4.2.6 as an exception.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-obx14-02
  • Warning 4.4.2.6 OBX-14 Date/Time of the Observation: SISC guidance Guidance on Populating OBX-14 (Guidance for specific observation types (table)): Partly covered. The guide gives the rule for serology and multi-day conditions, but not the table. Without it, senders must guess the date for eligibility, funding source, VIS, comments, evaluations, and forecasts.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance OBX-14 by observation: serological immunity, the date the specimen was collected; history of disease, the disease onset; eligibility, the date it was calculated; funding source, the date it was determined; VIS, the date presented; reaction, the date of the reaction; comment, the date entered or updated; evaluation (RSP), the date performed; forecast (RSP), the date generated.
    Recommendation Add the table to 4.4.2.6 (it is short), or put the date in each observation's row in Figure 20.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-obx14-06
  • Warning 4.7.8.7 Preferred and Contraindicated Vaccine Types: SISC guidance Guidance on Indicating Preferable and Contraindicated Vaccines (Scope of Guidance (In Scope)): Missing. The guide says these elements are used "cautiously and only when clinically justified", but not how a receiver should read them.
    MissingAction: Narrative
    Guidance Preferred vaccines sent in a response are not a complete list of possible products, and the clinician need not use one of them (though they must understand the implications of using a non-preferred vaccine).
    Recommendation Add that a preferred vaccine type is a recommendation, not a complete or mandatory list of products.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-pcv-01
  • Warning 4.7.8.7 Preferred and Contraindicated Vaccine Types: SISC guidance Guidance on Indicating Preferable and Contraindicated Vaccines (Expectations of Support): Partly covered. Both are MAY in the guide, so support is optional, and s4 asks IIS to document product guidance in their local IG. The safety warning is gone: a querying system that ignores unknown OBX segments is fine, but one that reads every CVX in a forecast set as recommended would show a contraindicated vaccine (93122-0) as due.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance Support is not required. The OBX segments are sent only when both trading partners agree there is value in it. A sender should not assume the receiver can handle them without discussion and testing; it is critical that a contraindicated vaccine is not mistaken for a recommended one. Partners may agree to exchange one kind but not the other.
    Recommendation State that IIS return these OBX segments only to querying systems that have agreed to receive them, and that querying systems SHALL NOT present a contraindicated vaccine type as recommended.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-pcv-02
  • Warning 4.8.6.3 Date Semantics: SISC guidance Guidance on Detailed Message Structure and the Use of Specific LOINC Codes (Change History (version 1.1)): Conflict. The guide's flavor sections follow this (30945-0 only in the Contraindicated group, which "links contraindication observations to a specific vaccine that was not given"), and so does IGAMT. But section 4.8.6.3 says conditions may be sent as standalone patient-level observations or in a not-administered group, that IIS process both "identically", and that RXA-20 = NA "does not create standardized meaning linking the condition to a specific non-administered vaccine". That reverses the reason for the v1.1 change. The representation conflict is already a finding (qf-igamt-condition-loinc); this adds the history CDC should weigh when deciding it.
    ConflictAction: CDC decision
    Guidance Contraindication observations were removed from the RSP Patient Observations group, because it is important to show the contraindicated vaccine in RXA-5. Contraindications should be sent in an Immunization Event (Contraindicated) group.
    Recommendation When deciding qf-igamt-condition-loinc, note that SISC moved contraindications into the Contraindicated group in 2018 precisely so the vaccine is identified. If the guide keeps 4.8.6.3, record the reversal in the change history.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-msl-12
  • Note 3.3.4 MSA-1: Derivation of Message-Level Disposition: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 Acknowledgement Messages to Support Interoperability (MSA-1 (Acknowledgement Code), Tables 4 and 5): Conflict. Changed on purpose, following the ACK TAB. In the guide, 'AE' SHALL NOT be returned when there is an Error, 'AR' is returned whenever core intent is not fulfilled (any Error), and "unable to process for reasons unrelated to format or content" is no longer a reason for 'AR'. The guide's MSA-1 guidance explains the change but does not name this document. IIS and senders that built to the 2015 rules will send or expect 'AE' for rejected messages.
    ConflictAction: Narrative
    Guidance MSA-1 is 'AA' when there are no ERR segments or only Information; 'AE' when at least one ERR segment is a Warning or an Error (so a rejected message also gets 'AE'); and 'AR' only when an Error is due to an unsupported message type, event code, or processing ID, or the message cannot be processed for reasons unrelated to format or content.
    Recommendation In the change history, record that the MSA-1 rules replace those in the 2015 ACK guidance (and Release 1.5): 'AE' no longer covers rejections. Consider a short migration note for systems that treat 'AE' as a rejection.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-ack-06
  • Note 3.5.2 RSP Response Profiles: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Query Response Guidance (query is successfully processed)): In narrative only. The guide describes the response profiles as the "controlled set of outcomes" of matching, and its Z33 section says that a Z33 means the IIS "has processed the query". It does not say outright that finding no patient, or several, is a successful query rather than an error.
    In narrative only
    Guidance A query that runs to completion is successful, however many patients it finds (none, one, or more than one). The Z31, Z32, Z33, or Z42 profile is used depending on the query profile and the outcome.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

The guide matches the profiles to these outcomes (figure 17 and the Z32, Z42, and Z33 sections) but does not give the QAK-2 values ('OK' and 'NF').
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance Profile and QAK-2 by outcome: no match, Z33 with 'NF'; one match, Z32 (to a Z34) or Z42 (to a Z44) with 'OK'.
    Recommendation Include the QAK-2 values when this is covered (see sisc-rsp-09).

  • Note 3.5.2.3 Z31 - Return a List of Candidates: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Summary (Table 2)): Conflict. The guide lets a Z31 answer a Z44 query as well, following the Query Response Flexibility TAB (January 2024), which replaced this part of the guidance. This change is intended. The IGAMT Z31 profile still requires QPD-1 'Z34', a conflict recorded in the TAB comparison (tab-qrf-04). The guide also does not give the QAK-2 value for a Z31 ('OK').
    ConflictAction: Narrative
    Guidance Multiple matches (fewer than the maximum allowed): to a Z34 query, Z31 with 'OK' or Z33 with 'NF'; to a Z44 query, Z33 with 'NF' or 'TM'.
    Recommendation Record in the change history that a Z31 may now answer a Z44 query, replacing this guidance, and give the QAK-2 value for a Z31.

  • Note 3.5.2.4 Z33 - Return a Response with No Person Records: SISC guidance Guidance for HL7 RSP Messages to Support Interoperability (Caveats; Summary): Partly covered. The IGAMT RSP profiles allow ERR [0..1], as the base standard requires, so the limit is enforced. The narrative never mentions it, or says which error to return when there are several. Readers who know the ACK (which allows many ERR segments) will not expect the difference.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance An RSP^K11 message may contain only a single ERR segment. When several errors are found, the responding system selects the most salient one to return.
    Recommendation Add a sentence to the RSP profile sections: an RSP carries at most one ERR segment, so the IIS returns the most significant issue.

Dates, counts, and dose validity match, and the DT_D flavor is kept. The CE/CWE inconsistency is already a finding (qf-igamt-c27-c28-ce-cwe).
    Reworded
    Guidance Coded observations use OBX-2 'CWE', dates 'DT' (formatted as the DT_D flavor), counts 'NM', and dose validity 'ID'.

A normal Z32 or Z42 for a protected patient whose data may be shared follows from that, but is not said.
    In narrative only

  • Note 4.6.2.4.3 NDC Requirements for Administered Vaccinations: SISC guidance Guidance on Unit of Sale/Unit of Use Lot Numbers (Notes: Matching, Reconstituted Vaccines, and Regular Expression): Partly covered. The guide requires the NDC of the lyophilized powder rather than the diluent, but does not say the same for the lot number. A submitter could report the diluent's lot with the powder's NDC.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance For reconstituted vaccines, the unit-of-sale lot and the lot on the lyophilized vaccine powder are usually the same; the liquid diluent typically has a different lot number.
    Recommendation Extend the reconstitution statement to the lot number: report the lot of the lyophilized powder (the active product), not the diluent.

  • Note 4.6.4.5 Inventory and Accountability Considerations: SISC guidance Guidance on Unit of Sale/Unit of Use Lot Numbers (Notes: Matching, Reconstituted Vaccines, and Regular Expression): Out of scope. The guide deliberately "does not prescribe an IIS's internal inventory or lot-validation workflows", so this is IIS operations. Its inventory section would still benefit from a link to this guidance and to the MIROW inventory guide.
    Out of scopeAction: Narrative
    Guidance Mapping a unit-of-sale lot to a unit-of-use lot is simple, but the reverse is not (except for Menveo). An IIS that knows a provider's inventory can usually match a reported unit-of-use lot to that provider's single unit-of-sale lot, and resolve any discrepancies during reconciliation (see the MIROW guide on decrementing inventory via electronic data exchange).
    Recommendation In the inventory section, link this guidance and the MIROW guide on decrementing inventory (see the MIROW alignment proposal).

    See the SISC guidance comparison

    qf-sisc-lot-02
  • Note 4.7.4 Overall Structure of CDS in the RSP: SISC guidance Guidance on Detailed Message Structure and the Use of Specific LOINC Codes (Structure of the Z32 and Z42 messages; Table 4): Partly covered. The guide's CDS rules match for any RSP with CDS content (evaluation in each history group, exactly one Forecast group, placed last), and the Forecast section requires a shared OBX-4 per set. But the guide has no summary of which flavors each profile uses, and does not say whether a Z32 may carry evaluation or a forecast. Figure 20 is organized by flavor, not by profile.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
    Guidance Which groups each profile uses (Table 4): Z22 uses Immunization Event (0..*), Patient Observations (0..1), Refused and Contraindicated (0..*). Z32 uses either Immunization Event or Immunization Event with Evaluation (the latter if a Forecast is present), an optional Forecast (0..1), and the rest as for Z22. Z42 uses Immunization Event with Evaluation (0..*), exactly one Forecast (1..1), and the rest as for Z22. Each forecast set in the Forecast group has its own OBX-4 sub-ID.
    Recommendation Add a short table like this guidance's Table 4 to section 4.4.4: each flavor against Z22, Z32, and Z42, with its cardinality.

    See the SISC guidance comparison

It does not say to skip it when only one product exists, or that the recommendation's dates apply to every preferred vaccine.
    Partly coveredAction: Narrative
